### 📄 ARQUIVO: ./DINGLI_EXPLICACOES.md
# DINGLI_EXPLICACOES.md
**Documentação Operacional, Arquitetura do Motor e Diretrizes Pedagógicas por Par - Dìnglì**

---

## 1. Visão Geral e Arquitetura da Esteira

O ecossistema de geração de explicações pedagógicas do Dìnglì opera de forma 100% autônoma na nuvem via **Google Apps Script (GAS)**, integrado diretamente à API REST do **Supabase** e com gravação no **Google Drive** (`Explicacoes_Prontas/`), eliminando a necessidade de terminal local ou máquina ligada durante a produção em lote.

### 1.1 O Ciclo de Execução em Lote Único
1. **Lote de 30 Frases:** Cada tópico do curso (composto por exatamente 30 frases) é enviado em uma **única chamada** à API do Gemini (`TAMANHO_BLOCO = 30`). O modelo processa e devolve o JSON completo com as 30 explicações de uma só vez, eliminando o antigo fracionamento 6x5 e economizando chamadas de rede.
2. **Modelo Exclusivo:** O pipeline utiliza estritamente o modelo **`gemini-3.5-flash`** com saída estruturada forçada via `responseMimeType: "application/json"` e `temperature: 0.2`. Não há uso de fallbacks para modelos secundários.
3. **Resiliência e Tolerância a Falhas:**
   - **Alternância de Chaves por Rodada:** O script alterna o uso das chaves (`GEMINI_API_KEYS`) a cada execução do acionador via `PropertiesService`, garantindo descanso total entre as rodadas.
   - **2 Tentativas Rápidas:** Executa no máximo 2 tentativas com intervalo de 1,5s por disparo. Em caso de erro (429 ou 503), encerra imediatamente em segundos, preservando a cota diária intacta para o próximo disparo.
   - **Trava de Segurança do Apps Script:** Monitoramento do tempo limite de segurança de 5 minutos (`300000 ms`) para evitar interrupções forçadas pelo Google.
4. **Auto-Recuperação na Planilha:** Na inicialização de cada rodada, o script varre a planilha e resgata qualquer linha que tenha ficado travada em `Em Processamento` ou com erro, resetando seu status automaticamente para `Pendente` com preenchimento em vermelho claro (`#f4cccc`).

### 1.2 Dimensionamento de Frequência e Cotas
- **Tempo médio de processamento:** ~55 a 65 segundos por tópico (30 frases).
- **Gatilho de 15 minutos:** Processa ~96 tópicos/dia (~2.880 frases/dia). Atinge o teto diário de 90 minutos de execução de gatilhos do Apps Script (contas `@gmail.com`) no final da noite, entregando entre 85 e 90 tópicos concluídos diariamente.
- **Gatilho de 30 minutos:** Processa 48 tópicos/dia (~1.440 frases/dia), consumindo ~48 minutos diários do Apps Script e mantendo folga total de tokens para concorrência com processamentos paralelos de aulas.

---

## 2. Modelagem de Dados no Supabase

O motor não utiliza regras estáticas em arquivos `.gs`. Toda a inteligência pedagógica, regras fonéticas e exemplos mestres são consumidos dinamicamente do Supabase via REST API.

### 2.1 Tabela `sentences`
Armazena o acervo central de frases do aplicativo:
- `id`: Identificador numérico único da frase.
- `level`: Nível de proficiência CEFR (`A1`, `A2`, `B1`, `B2`).
- `topic_[origem]`: Nome do tópico na língua materna do aluno (ex: `topic_pt`).
- Colunas de idioma: `en`, `pt`, `es`, `fr`, `it`, `ge` (e colunas específicas `zh` e `pi` para mandarim).
- **Endpoint de consulta:**
  `GET /rest/v1/sentences?level=eq.{nivel}&topic_{origem}=eq.{topico}&order=id.asc`

### 2.2 Tabela `matriz_pares`
Centraliza a parametrização didática e fonética de cada par de idiomas:
- `par` (Chave Primária): Código do par no formato `estudo_origem` (ex: `en_pt`, `pi_pt`, `fr_pt`, `es_pt`, `it_pt`, `ge_pt`).
- `nome_estudo`: Nome por extenso do idioma que está sendo ensinado (ex: `Inglês`, `Mandarim (Pinyin)`).
- `nome_origem`: Nome por extenso do idioma nativo do aluno (ex: `Português`).
- `instrucao_geral`: Prompt base com a persona do professor, regras rígidas de formatação dos campos e proibições terminológicas.
- `regras_foneticas`: O compêndio numerado de regras fonéticas aplicáveis àquele idioma sob a perspectiva da L1.
- `exemplo_mestre` (JSONB): Objeto JSON contendo a Frase Mestra Universal (Frase 1171) resolvida no padrão ouro para o par.
- **Endpoint de consulta:**
  `GET /rest/v1/matriz_pares?par=eq.{par}&select=nome_estudo,nome_origem,instrucao_geral,regras_foneticas,exemplo_mestre`

---

## 3. Diretrizes Pedagógicas Universais

Toda explicação pedagógica gerada deve seguir o tom de um amigo professor experiente, claro, acolhedor e totalmente livre de academicismos.

### 3.1 Proibição Absoluta de Jargões
- **Termos Gramaticais Banidos:** canônica, sintagma, elisão, clítico, próclise, ênclise, anafórico, deítico, pretérito, acusativo, dativo, genitivo, declinação, desinência, partícula aspectual, classificador nominal, cópula, oração subordinada, particípio, transitividade, adjunto adverbial, phrasal verb, conector subordinativo.
- **Termos Fonéticos Banidos:** fricativa, oclusiva, bilabial, alveolar, velar, glotal, liaison, schwa, flap, apócope, fonema, alofone, IPA.

### 3.2 Estrutura Rigorosa dos 3 Campos Gerados
Cada objeto de frase no JSON final deve conter estritamente as três chaves:

1. **`explanation` (A Lógica Mental da Frase):**
   - Parágrafo único contínuo (estritamente sem quebras de linha `\n`).
   - Revela como o nativo da L2 pensa a ideia em comparação direta com a L1, explicando ordem das palavras, conexões naturais e o sentido prático real (evitando traduções literais truncadas).
2. **`breakdown` (O Raio-X do Vocabulário):**
   - Array de objetos `[{"word": "...", "meaning": "..."}]`.
   - O campo `word` **nunca** deve conter pontuação no final (`?`, `.`, `!`, `,`).
   - Decompõe blocos significativos, termos compostos e preposições, detalhando cada pedaço e seu papel no sentido geral.
3. **`pronuncia` (Instruções Físicas + Transcrição Imediata):**
   - Texto corrido orientando a mecânica física da fala através dos 4 passos táteis:
     1. Ponto de contato (onde posicionar língua, dentes e lábios).
     2. Saída do ar e da voz (ar livre, sopro morno, retenção ou vibração).
     3. Trava da L1 (qual vício automático da língua materna deve ser bloqueado).
     4. Analogia sensorial simples do cotidiano.
   - Fechamento obrigatório na última linha com o comando da língua nativa:
     `Leia: [transcrição fonética adaptada]`

### 3.3 A Frase Mestra Universal (Frase 1171)
Para garantir coerência analítica e comparabilidade entre todos os 42 pares do Dìnglì, a **Frase 1171** (*O conceito de globalização alterou fundamentalmente a maneira como vivemos*) é o padrão ouro oficial adotado no Supabase para todos os pares homologados.

---

## 4. Travas Fonéticas por Par Homologado (L1 Português)

### 4.1 Inglês (`en_pt`)
- **4 Símbolos Obrigatórios com Instrução Tátil:**
  - `ı` (I curto): Queixo solto, sem sorriso, som rápido e frouxo entre 'e' e 'i' (`ship`, `live`).
  - `æ` (A aberto rasgado): Mandíbula aberta na vertical em 'A', cantos dos lábios puxados para os lados em 'É' (`cat`, `apple`).
  - `ə` (Som neutro): Mandíbula caída e língua relaxada, sopro curtíssimo e sem força (`about`, `problem`).
  - `3r` (Vogal do R central): Língua curvada para trás no céu da boca sem tocar em nada (`bird`, `work`).
- **Pares TH:** `th` surdo (língua entre os dentes assoprando ar puro) vs `dh` sonoro (língua entre os dentes vibrando a voz).
- **Consoantes Finais e S Mudo:** Consoantes finais cortam a seco sem 'i' de apoio (`stop`, `book`). Palavras com S inicial seguido de consoante começam direto no silvo do S, sem 'is-' ou 'es-' (`speak`, `study`).
- **Dark L e M Final:** No L final, a ponta da língua cola no céu da boca sem virar som de 'U' (`milk`, `hotel`). No M final, os lábios selam juntos a seco (`time`, `room`).

### 4.2 Mandarim Pinyin (`pi_pt`)
- **Proibição Estrita de 'E/Ê' em Som Gutural e Tom Neutro:** É terminantemente proibido grafar o som gutural posterior (/ɤ/) ou o tom neutro com 'e' ou 'ê'. Grafa-se obrigatoriamente: `da`, `la`, `ga`, `ka`, `rra` e `chán-ma` (para *shénme*).
- **Oposição de Aspiração:** B, D, G, Z, ZH e J são estalos secos sem ar e sem voz (som de P, T, C/K secos). Já P, T, K, C, CH e Q disparam um sopro explosivo de ar quente.
- **Série Retroflexa vs Palatal:** ZH, CH, SH e R articulam com a ponta da língua curvada para trás no céu da boca. J, Q e X articulam com sorriso tenso e língua espalmada na frente.
- **Vogal Ü de Bico:** Em *ju*, *qu*, *xu*, *yu*, lábios em bico firme de 'U' emitindo som de 'I' agudo por dentro, grafado com trema: `djü`, `tchü`, `xü`, `yü`.
- **Rimas com Vogal Oculta:** `-IAN` fecha em `iên`; `-IU` carrega 'o' oculto (`iôu`); `-UI` carrega 'e' central (`uêi`); `-UN` soa `uên`; `BO/PO/MO/FO` soam com transição labial em `uô`.
- **Corte Nasal Dental (-n) vs Ressonância Velar (-ng):** Em `-n` a ponta da língua trava atrás dos dentes da frente; em `-ng` a língua repousa embaixo e o dorso veda o fundo do céu da boca mole.

### 4.3 Francês (`fr_pt`)
- **4 Símbolos Obrigatórios com Instrução Tátil:**
  - `ü` (U francês): Lábios em bico apertado de assobio dizendo 'I' por dentro (`tu`, `rue`).
  - `ø` (EU fechado): Lábios em bico redondo de 'Ô' dizendo 'Ê' fechado por dentro (`peu`, `deux`).
  - `œ` (EU aberto): Lábios arredondados em 'Ó' dizendo 'É' aberto por dentro (`peur`, `sœur`).
  - `ə` (E mudo/relaxado): Lábios levemente arredondados, boca solta e som curto (`le`, `de`).
- **R de Garganta:** Raspar suavemente o fundo da garganta, sem vibrar a ponta da língua e sem raspar áspero (`paris`, `rouge`).
- **Vogais Nasais Puras:** Proibição de bater a língua ou fechar os lábios no final. O ar sai 100% pelo nariz: `ón` (an/am/en/em), `on` (on/om), `in` (in/im/ain/ein).
- **Consoantes Finais Mudas:** As letras D, T, P, S, X e Z no final das palavras morrem silenciosas.

### 4.4 Espanhol (`es_pt`)
- **Vogais Finais Fechadas:** O 'E' final nunca vira 'I' e o 'O' final nunca vira 'U'. Sustentam-se cravados em `ê` e `ô` (`nô-tche`, `a-mí-go`).
- **Proibição Total do Som de 'Z' (Abelha):** A letra S entre vogais é sempre sibilante e seca (`ka-sa` soando "kássa"). As letras Z e C (antes de E/I) soam estritamente como S puro internacional (`sa-pa-tô`, `kô-ra-són`).
- **J e G (antes de E/I) como Suspiro Aspirado:** Suspiro suave e morno direto do peito pela boca aberta (baforar no espelho), grafado estritamente com `h` (`ha-món`, `hên-tê`, `í-hô`). Proibido usar 'j' ou raspar garganta.
- **RR Vibrante na Ponta da Língua:** Tremor mecânico da ponta da língua no céu da boca ("motorzinho: brrr"), proibindo raspar a garganta.
- **Fusão B/V:** B e V têm o mesmo som labial suave, sem encostar dentes no lábio inferior.
- **Voz 100% Oral:** Toda vogal antes de N/M sai totalmente limpa pela boca antes do toque consonantal (`pan` sai oral limpo antes do toque dental, nunca "pão").

### 4.5 Italiano (`it_pt`)
- **Consoantes Duplas / Geminadas:** Reter o ar e segurar a musculatura pelo dobro do tempo na consoante dobrada antes de liberar a vogal seguinte (`pál-la`, `nót-te`, `kôn-tchét-to`).
- **Família do C e G:** C antes de E/I tem estalo de `tch` (`tcháo`); CH antes de E/I é K duro (`per-kê`). G antes de E/I tem estalo de `dj` (`dje-lá-to`); GH antes de E/I é G duro de gato (`spa-guét-ti`).
- **Família do SC:** SC antes de E/I é chiado contínuo suave `ch` (`chê-na`); SCH antes de E/I quebra o chiado e vira `sk` duro (`brus-két-ta`).
- **Letra Z e ZZ Africadas:** Nunca tem som de abelha frouxo. É estalo seco `ts` (`pít-tsa`, `grát-tsie`) ou sonoro `dz` (`dzê-ro`).
- **Proibição de Redução Vocálica:** Vogais finais soam completas, abertas e firmes (`nót-te`, `a-mí-ko`), sem anasalar antes de N/M.
- **T e D Secos:** Ponta da língua reta atrás dos dentes sem chiar como 'tchi' ou 'dji' (`ti á-mo`, `mat-tí-na`).

### 4.6 Alemão (`ge_pt`)
- **Umlauts Ö e Ü:**
  - `ö` / `öö`: Língua posicionada para falar 'Ê' ou 'É', lábios projetados em bico redondo de 'Ô' ou 'Ó' (`schön`, `können`).
  - `ü` / `üü`: Lábios em bico fechado e firme de 'U', língua na frente para dizer 'I' agudo (`Tür`, `fünf`).
- **Inversão Crítica W vs V:** A letra W soa obrigatoriamente como 'V' do português (`Wasser` -> `vás-sa`). A letra V nativa soa como 'F' (`Vater` -> `fáa-ta`, `viel` -> `fíil`).
- **O 'R' Vocalizado:** No final `-er` e após vogais longas, não se raspa a garganta: morre como um 'a' curto e relaxado (`Wasser` -> `vás-sa`, `wir` -> `víia`).
- **Desvozeamento Final (Auslautverhärtung):** No final de palavras ou sílabas, B vira P seco, D vira T seco e G vira K seco (`ab` -> `ap`, `und` -> `unt`, `Tag` -> `táak`).
- **Golpe de Glote (Knacklaut):** Palavras iniciadas por vogal exigem um microbloqueio na laringe, nascendo com ataque seco independente, grafado com apóstrofo no *Leia::* (`ein Apfel` -> `áin 'áp-fel`).
- **Letra Z:** Soa estritamente como estalo seco `ts` colado (`Zeit` -> `tsáit`), sem vibrar como abelha.

---

## 5. Infraestrutura no Google Drive e Planilha de Controle

### 5.1 Estrutura de Pastas
```text
[Google Drive]
└── Dingli_Gerador_Explicacoes/
    ├── Explicacoes_Prontas/       # Arquivos finais gerados (ex: A1_Comida e Restaurantes.json)
    └── Controle_Geracao           # Planilha Google com a fila de execução e auditoria
```

### 5.2 Colunas da Planilha `Controle_Geracao`
- **Coluna A:** `ID_Lote` (Identificador numérico sequencial)
- **Coluna B:** `Par` (Código do par cadastrado no Supabase, ex: `en_pt`, `pi_pt`)
- **Coluna C:** `Nivel` (Nível CEFR: `A1`, `A2`, `B1`, `B2`)
- **Coluna D:** `Topico` (Nome do tópico a processar)
- **Coluna E:** `Status` (`Pendente`, `Em Processamento`, `Concluído`, `Pendente` [#f4cccc])
- **Coluna F:** `Data_Inicio` (Data e hora do disparo)
- **Coluna G:** `Duracao` (Tempo total gasto ou descrição do erro capturado)
- **Coluna H:** `Nome_Arquivo_Final` (Nome do JSON criado na pasta pronta)

---

## 6. Código Oficial de Produção

### 6.1 `Config.gs`
```javascript
const CONFIG = {
  GEMINI_API_KEYS: [
    "SUA_CHAVE_API_GEMINI_1",
    "SUA_CHAVE_API_GEMINI_2"
  ],
  GEMINI_MODELS: ["gemini-3.5-flash"],
  SUPABASE_URL: "https://SEU_PROJETO.supabase.co",
  SUPABASE_KEY: "SUA_SERVICE_ROLE_OU_ANON_KEY",
  ID_PLANILHA_CONTROLE: "ID_DA_PLANILHA_GOOGLE",
  ID_PASTA_PRONTAS: "ID_DA_PASTA_EXPLICACOES_PRONTAS_NO_DRIVE"
};
```

### 6.2 `Motor.gs`
```javascript
function chamarGeminiCascata(payload, tInicio) {
  const modelCandidate = CONFIG.GEMINI_MODELS[0];
  const chaves = CONFIG.GEMINI_API_KEYS || [CONFIG.GEMINI_API_KEY];
  const props = PropertiesService.getScriptProperties();

  // Alterna a chave inicial da rodada com base na última utilizada com sucesso
  let lastIndex = parseInt(props.getProperty("LAST_USED_KEY_INDEX") || "1", 10);
  let startIndex = (lastIndex + 1) % chaves.length;

  // Sequência alternada de 4 tentativas no total: A -> B -> A -> B
  for (let attempt = 1; attempt <= 4; attempt++) {
    const keyIndex = (startIndex + (attempt - 1)) % chaves.length;
    const chaveAtual = chaves[keyIndex];
    const numChave = keyIndex + 1;
    const url = `https://generativelanguage.googleapis.com/v1beta/models/${modelCandidate}:generateContent?key=${encodeURIComponent(chaveAtual)}`;

    Logger.log(`[GEMINI] Tentativa ${attempt}/4 com Chave ${numChave}/${chaves.length}...`);

    try {
      const response = UrlFetchApp.fetch(url, {
        method: "post",
        contentType: "application/json",
        payload: JSON.stringify(payload),
        muteHttpExceptions: true
      });

      const code = response.getResponseCode();

      if (code === 200) {
        const json = JSON.parse(response.getContentText());
        let rawText = json.candidates[0].content.parts[0].text.trim();
        rawText = rawText.replace(/^```json\s*/i, "").replace(/```$/i, "").trim();
        rawText = rawText.replace(/,(\s*[\}\]])/g, "$1");

        Logger.log(`[GEMINI SUCESSO] Resposta gerada via Chave ${numChave}.`);
        props.setProperty("LAST_USED_KEY_INDEX", String(keyIndex));
        return JSON.parse(rawText);
      }

      if (code === 400) {
        Logger.log(`[GEMINI 400] Erro de sintaxe/payload: ${response.getContentText()}`);
        return null;
      }

      Logger.log(`[GEMINI] Chave ${numChave} retornou HTTP ${code} na tentativa ${attempt}.`);
    } catch (e) {
      Logger.log(`[GEMINI] Falha na Chave ${numChave} (Tentativa ${attempt}): ${e.message}`);
    }

    if (attempt < 4) {
      Utilities.sleep(3000);
    }
  }

  // Se esgotar as 4 tentativas alternadas, grava o índice de partida para a próxima rodada
  props.setProperty("LAST_USED_KEY_INDEX", String(startIndex));
  Logger.log("[GEMINI ERRO] Ambas as chaves esgotaram as 4 tentativas alternadas. A encerrar sem queimar quota.");
  return null;
}

function obterFrasesSupabase(par, nivel, topico) {
  const partes = par.split("_");
  const estudo = partes[0];
  const origem = partes[1];
  const colTopicOrigem = `topic_${origem}`;

  const url = `${CONFIG.SUPABASE_URL}/rest/v1/sentences?level=eq.${encodeURIComponent(nivel)}&${colTopicOrigem}=eq.${encodeURIComponent(topico)}&order=id.asc`;
  const options = {
    method: "get",
    headers: {
      "apikey": CONFIG.SUPABASE_KEY,
      "Authorization": `Bearer ${CONFIG.SUPABASE_KEY}`,
      "Accept": "application/json"
    },
    muteHttpExceptions: true
  };

  const response = UrlFetchApp.fetch(url, options);
  if (response.getResponseCode() !== 200) {
    throw new Error(`Falha ao consultar frases no Supabase: HTTP ${response.getResponseCode()}`);
  }

  const dados = JSON.parse(response.getContentText());
  const ehMandarim = (estudo === "zh" || estudo === "pi");

  return dados.map(f => {
    const item = {
      id: f.id,
      [origem]: f[origem]
    };
    if (ehMandarim) {
      item.zh = f.zh;
      item.pi = f.pi;
    } else {
      item[estudo] = f[estudo];
    }
    return item;
  });
}

function obterMatrizSupabase(par) {
  const url = `${CONFIG.SUPABASE_URL}/rest/v1/matriz_pares?par=eq.${encodeURIComponent(par)}&select=nome_estudo,nome_origem,instrucao_geral,regras_foneticas,exemplo_mestre`;
  const options = {
    method: "get",
    headers: {
      "apikey": CONFIG.SUPABASE_KEY,
      "Authorization": `Bearer ${CONFIG.SUPABASE_KEY}`,
      "Accept": "application/json"
    },
    muteHttpExceptions: true
  };

  const response = UrlFetchApp.fetch(url, options);
  if (response.getResponseCode() !== 200) {
    throw new Error(`Falha ao consultar matriz no Supabase: HTTP ${response.getResponseCode()} - ${response.getContentText()}`);
  }

  const dados = JSON.parse(response.getContentText());
  if (!dados || dados.length === 0) {
    throw new Error(`Par '${par}' não encontrado na tabela matriz_pares do Supabase.`);
  }

  return dados[0];
}

function processarProximoTopico() {
  const planilha = SpreadsheetApp.openById(CONFIG.ID_PLANILHA_CONTROLE).getActiveSheet();
  const dados = planilha.getDataRange().getValues();

  // 1. Resgate no início: linhas que não sejam "Concluído" nem "Pendente" viram "Pendente" em vermelho (#f4cccc)
  for (let i = 1; i < dados.length; i++) {
    const statusAtual = String(dados[i][4]).trim();
    const statusLower = statusAtual.toLowerCase();

    if (statusAtual !== "" && statusLower !== "concluído" && statusLower !== "pendente" && statusLower !== "erro") {
      planilha.getRange(i + 1, 5).setValue("Pendente").setBackground("#f4cccc");
      if (statusLower.includes("processamento")) {
        planilha.getRange(i + 1, 7).setValue("Interrompido / Timeout anterior");
      }
      dados[i][4] = "Pendente";
    }
  }

  // 2. Busca a próxima pendência
  let linhaAlvo = -1;
  let tarefa = null;

  for (let i = 1; i < dados.length; i++) {
    if (dados[i][4] === "Pendente") {
      linhaAlvo = i + 1;
      tarefa = {
        idLote: dados[i][0],
        par: dados[i][1],
        nivel: dados[i][2],
        topico: dados[i][3]
      };
      break;
    }
  }

  if (!tarefa) {
    Logger.log("Nenhuma tarefa com status 'Pendente' encontrada.");
    return;
  }

  const tInicio = new Date();
  const fInicio = Utilities.formatDate(tInicio, Session.getScriptTimeZone(), "dd/MM/yyyy HH:mm:ss");

  planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 5).setValue("Em Processamento").setBackground(null);
  planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 6).setValue(fInicio);
  planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 7).setValue("");

  try {
    Logger.log(`Buscando matriz de regras no Supabase para o par '${tarefa.par}'...`);
    const matriz = obterMatrizSupabase(tarefa.par);

    Logger.log(`Buscando frases do Supabase para [${tarefa.nivel}] ${tarefa.topico} (${tarefa.par})...`);
    const frasesLote = obterFrasesSupabase(tarefa.par, tarefa.nivel, tarefa.topico);

    if (!frasesLote || frasesLote.length === 0) {
      throw new Error("Nenhuma frase retornada pelo Supabase para este tópico.");
    }

    const TAMANHO_BLOCO = 30;
    let jsonFinal = {};

    for (let b = 0; b < frasesLote.length; b += TAMANHO_BLOCO) {
      if (new Date() - tInicio > 300000) {
        throw new Error("Tempo limite de segurança (5 minutos) atingido");
      }

      const blocoFrases = frasesLote.slice(b, b + TAMANHO_BLOCO);

      Logger.log(`Processando ${blocoFrases.length} frases do tópico (ID ${blocoFrases[0].id} a ${blocoFrases[blocoFrases.length - 1].id})...`);

      const prompt = `${matriz.instrucao_geral}

${matriz.regras_foneticas}

FORMATO DE RETORNO EXCLUSIVO (JSON puro):
{
  "ID_DA_FRASE": {
    "explanation": "...",
    "breakdown": [
      { "word": "...", "meaning": "..." }
    ],
    "pronuncia": "...\nLeia: ..."
  }
}

EXEMPLO DE PADRÃO OURO:
${JSON.stringify({ "1171": matriz.exemplo_mestre }, null, 2)}

LOTE DE FRASES DO TÓPICO A PROCESSAR:
${JSON.stringify(blocoFrases, null, 2)}`;

      const payload = {
        contents: [{ parts: [{ text: prompt }] }],
        generationConfig: {
          responseMimeType: "application/json",
          temperature: 0.2
        }
      };

      const jsonResultado = chamarGeminiCascata(payload, tInicio);

      if (!jsonResultado) {
        throw new Error(`Falha na API no lote de frases.`);
      }

      let blocoNormalizado = jsonResultado;
      if (Array.isArray(jsonResultado)) {
        blocoNormalizado = {};
        jsonResultado.forEach(item => Object.assign(blocoNormalizado, item));
      } else if (typeof jsonResultado === 'object' && jsonResultado !== null) {
        const chaves = Object.keys(jsonResultado);
        if (chaves.length === 1 && typeof jsonResultado[chaves[0]] === 'object' && !jsonResultado[String(blocoFrases[0].id)]) {
          const primeiroValor = jsonResultado[chaves[0]];
          if (Array.isArray(primeiroValor)) {
            blocoNormalizado = {};
            primeiroValor.forEach(item => Object.assign(blocoNormalizado, item));
          } else {
            blocoNormalizado = primeiroValor;
          }
        }
      }

      Object.assign(jsonFinal, blocoNormalizado);

      if (b + TAMANHO_BLOCO < frasesLote.length) {
        Utilities.sleep(30000);
      }
    }

    const totalGeradas = Object.keys(jsonFinal).length;
    if (totalGeradas !== frasesLote.length) {
      throw new Error(`Inconsistência de frases: esperava ${frasesLote.length}, mas foram geradas ${totalGeradas}.`);
    }

    const nomeArquivoFinal = `${tarefa.nivel}_${tarefa.topico}.json`;
    const pastaProntas = DriveApp.getFolderById(CONFIG.ID_PASTA_PRONTAS);
    pastaProntas.createFile(nomeArquivoFinal, JSON.stringify(jsonFinal, null, 2), MimeType.PLAIN_TEXT);

    const duracaoSegundos = Math.round((new Date() - tInicio) / 1000);
    const min = Math.floor(duracaoSegundos / 60);
    const seg = duracaoSegundos % 60;
    const duracaoFormatada = min > 0 ? `${min}m ${seg}s` : `${seg}s`;

    planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 5).setValue("Concluído").setBackground(null);
    planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 7).setValue(duracaoFormatada);
    planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 8).setValue(nomeArquivoFinal);

    Logger.log(`✔ Tópico concluído com sucesso: ${nomeArquivoFinal} em ${duracaoFormatada}`);

  } catch (err) {
    Logger.log(`Falha ao processar: ${err.message}`);
    const duracaoSegundos = Math.round((new Date() - tInicio) / 1000);
    const min = Math.floor(duracaoSegundos / 60);
    const seg = duracaoSegundos % 60;
    const duracaoFormatada = min > 0 ? `${min}m ${seg}s` : `${seg}s`;

   const ehErroDados = err.message.includes("Nenhuma frase retornada") || err.message.includes("não encontrado");
    const novoStatus = ehErroDados ? "Erro" : "Pendente";

    planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 5).setValue(novoStatus).setBackground("#f4cccc");
    planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 7).setValue(`Erro: ${err.message} (${duracaoFormatada})`);
  } finally {
    // 3. Checagem final garantida: qualquer status diferente de "Concluído" e "Erro" permanece como "Pendente" (#f4cccc)
    const statusFinal = planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 5).getValue();
    if (statusFinal !== "Concluído" && statusFinal !== "Erro") {
      planilha.getRange(linhaAlvo, 5).setValue("Pendente").setBackground("#f4cccc");
    }
  }
}
```

### 📄 ARQUIVO: ./DINGLI_EXPLICACOES_L1EN.md
pt_en
1. Unstressed Final Vowels (-E, -O, -A) vs. Clear Pre-Stressed Vowels (noite, amigo, casa, banana, comer)
How to do it: In Brazilian Portuguese, unstressed vowels at the very end of a word soften predictably: final "-e" sounds like a crisp "ee" (noite), final "-o" sounds like a short "oo" (amigo), and final "-a" relaxes into a light, neutral "uh" (casa). However, vowels that appear before the stressed syllable stay clear and open, never turning into a lazy neutral sound (banana starts with an open "bah", and comer keeps a clear "koh").
L1 Trap: Native English speakers automatically reduce unstressed vowels before the stress into a neutral "uh" sound ("buh-nah-nuh", "kuh-mer") and tend to pronounce final "-e" and "-o" as heavy "eh" or "oh". Keep early syllables clear and let only the final syllable soften.
Spelling in Read:: ee for final -e (nôy-tchee), oo for final -o (ah-mee-goo), uh for final -a (kah-zuh), and clear vowels before the stress (bah-nah-nuh, koh-mêr).

2. Pure Steady Vowels vs. English Gliding Vowels (Ê and Ô) (você, mesa, avô, bolo)
How to do it: Hold your lips and tongue completely frozen from the start of the vowel until the sound stops. Closed "ê" (você, mesa) and closed "ô" (avô, bolo) are pure, single-note steady sounds with zero movement at the end.
L1 Trap: English speakers naturally slide the jaw and lips upward at the end of long vowels, turning "ê" into "ay-ee" (like in "say") and "ô" into "oh-oo" (like in "go"). Saying "voh-say-ee" or "ah-voh-oo" immediately sounds foreign; freeze your mouth on one steady note.
Spelling in Read:: ê for steady closed E (voh-sê, mê-zuh) and ô for steady closed O (ah-vô, bô-loo).

3. Open vs. Closed Mid Vowels (É vs. Ê and Ó vs. Ô) (café, pé, avó, porta vs. você, avô, bolo)
How to do it: Portuguese separates two clear mouth openings for E and O:
Open sounds (eh, aw): drop your jaw vertically to make a wide, open "eh" as in "bed" (café, pé) and a wide, open "aw" as in "law" (avó, porta).
Closed sounds (ê, ô): keep a smaller, tighter mouth opening with tense lips for a steady "ê" (você) and a round, steady "ô" (avô, bolo).
L1 Trap: Switching open and closed vowels changes word meanings completely: avó (open "aw") means grandmother, while avô (closed "ô") means grandfather; pode (open "aw") means "can", while pôde (closed "ô") means "could".
Spelling in Read:: eh and aw for open sounds (kah-feh, peh, ah-vaw, pawr-tuh) vs. ê and ô for closed sounds (voh-sê, ah-vô, bô-loo).

4. Pure Nasal Vowels Without Closing the Mouth (Ã, Ẽ, Ĩ, Õ, Ũ) (bom, sim, tempo, mundo, irmã)
How to do it: When a vowel is followed by "m" or "n" at the end of a syllable or word, or carries a tilde (~), leave your mouth open and send the air and voice out through both your mouth and nose at the same time. The "m" or "n" is never pronounced as a consonant—it only signals that the vowel itself is nasal.
L1 Trap: English speakers instinctively press their lips together for "m" ("bawm", "tehm-poh") or press the tongue tip behind the top teeth for "n" ("moon-doh"). In Portuguese, neither the lips nor the tongue tip ever touch; the sound ends openly in the air.
Spelling in Read:: ã, ẽ, ĩ, õ, ũ with no m or n after them (bõ, sĩ, tẽ-poo, mũ-doo, eer-mã).

5. Nasal Diphthongs and Unstressed Verb Endings (-ÃO/-AM, -ÃE, -EM, -ÕE) (não, falam, mãe, bem, ontem, põe)
How to do it: Start with a nasal vowel and glide smoothly into a nasal "w" or "y" without ever closing your lips or touching your tongue to the roof of your mouth:
- "-ão" and unstressed verb "-am" share the same nasal "ãw" glide, differing only in which syllable carries the stress (stressed "nãw" vs. first-syllable stress in "FAH-lãw" for falam).
- "-ãe" starts open ("ãy", as in mãe).
- Final "-em" starts with a closed nasal "ẽ" before gliding ("ẽy", as in bem and ontem).
- "-õe" starts with a rounded nasal "õ" before gliding ("õy", as in põe).
L1 Trap: English speakers close their lips on "-am" ("fah-lahm"), leave "-em" non-nasal ("ehm"), or shift the stress onto the final syllable of verbs ("fah-LÃW" instead of "FAH-lãw"). Keep your mouth open on every nasal glide and keep verb "-am" unstressed.
Spelling in Read:: ãw for -ão and unstressed -am with stress marked (nãw, FAH-lãw), ãy for -ãe (mãy), ẽy for -em (bẽy, Õ-tẽy), and õy for -õe (põy).

6. Automatic Vowel Closing and Nasalization Before M and N (cama, banana, semana, nome, fome)
How to do it: In Brazilian Portuguese, whenever a stressed "a", "e", or "o" comes right before "m" or "n" in the next syllable, the vowel automatically closes and picks up a gentle nasal resonance through the nose, even without a tilde (~). So "a" becomes a closed nasal "ã" (cama, banana, semana), "e" becomes a closed nasal "ẽ" (cena, pequeno), and "o" becomes a closed nasal "õ" (nome, fome, telefone).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers pronounce these vowels wide open and completely oral, saying "KAH-muh" for cama, "buh-NAH-nuh" for banana, or "NAW-mee" for nome. In Portuguese, opening the vowel before M or N sounds harsh and unnatural; let the sound close and resonate softly through your nose before your lips or tongue touch the M or N.
Spelling in Read:: ã, ẽ, õ before m or n (KÃ-muh, bah-NÃ-nuh, seh-MÃ-nuh, NÕ-mee, FÕ-mee).

7. Open vs. Closed Oral Vowel Glides (ÉU/EU, ÓI/OI, ÉI/EI) and Natural Smoothing of OU and EI (céu/seu, herói/noite, papéis/sei, pouco, primeiro)
How to do it: When two oral vowels glide together in one syllable, pay attention to whether the first vowel starts open or closed:
- Open glides (accented): drop your jaw wide for open "eh" or "aw" before gliding into "w" or "y" (céu = sehw, herói = eh-RAWY, papéis = pah-PEHYS).
- Closed glides (unaccented): keep the starting vowel tight and closed (seu = sêw, noite = NÔY-tchee, sei = sêy).
- Everyday smoothing: in natural Brazilian speech, "ou" simplifies to a single steady closed "ô" (pouco = PÔ-koo, vou = vô), and "ei" before "r", "x", or "j" smooths into a single steady "ê" (primeiro = pree-MÊ-roo, peixe = PÊ-shee).
L1 Trap: English speakers pronounce "ou" as a heavy double-vowel glide ("POH-oo-koh") and miss the meaning difference between open and closed glides—for example, céu (open "sehw", sky) vs. seu (closed "sêw", your).
Spelling in Read:: ehw, awy, ehys for open glides (sehw, eh-RAWY, pah-PEHYS); êw, ôy, êy for closed glides (sêw, NÔY-tchee, sêy); and single ô or ê where smoothed (PÔ-koo, pree-MÊ-roo, PÊ-shee).

8. Separate Vowels in Sequence Without Merging (saúde, saída, país, dia, rua)
How to do it: When two vowels sit side by side—especially when the second vowel ("i" or "u") carries a written accent mark—give each vowel its own separate syllable beat instead of blending them into a single glide. Keep your voice steady and transition cleanly from one vowel to the next (saúde = sah-OO-djee, saída = sah-EE-duh, país = pah-EES).
L1 Trap: English speakers instinctively squash adjacent vowels into one quick syllable, turning país (country) into "pays" (which means parents, pais) or merging saúde into two syllables instead of three. Always give an accented "í" or "ú" its own full beat.
Spelling in Read:: Hyphen separating the two vowel beats (sah-OO-djee, sah-EE-duh, pah-EES, DJEE-uh, RHOO-uh).

9. The Strong "R" (RR, Word-Initial R, and R After N/L/S) (carro, rua, rápido, genro, Israel)
How to do it: When "r" starts a word (rua, rápido), is doubled in the middle (carro, terra), or follows "n", "l", or "s" (genro, Israel), do not curl your tongue tip upward. Keep the tip of your tongue resting flat behind your bottom teeth and release a soft, breathy puff of air from the back of your throat or chest—very similar to the English "h" in "house" or a gentle throat friction.
L1 Trap: Native English speakers automatically curl the tongue tip back for every written "r", turning carro and rua into an American-style "car-roh" and "roo-uh". In Brazilian Portuguese, an initial or doubled "r" never uses the curled English tongue position; it is always an airy throat/chest breath.
Spelling in Read:: rh (KAH-rhoo, RHOO-uh, RHAH-pee-doo, ZHẼ-rhoo, eez-rhah-EHL).

10. The Soft Single "R" Between Vowels and in Consonant Clusters (caro, arara, prato, trabalho, obrigado)
How to do it: When a single "r" sits between two vowels (caro, arara, agora) or right after another consonant in the same syllable (prato, trabalho, obrigado), flick the very tip of your tongue once against the ridge just behind your upper front teeth. It is the exact same quick tongue tap Americans use for the "tt" or "dd" in "water", "butter", or "ladder".
L1 Trap: English speakers curl their tongue back and pull it into the middle of the mouth ("KAH-roh", "PRAH-toh"). If you curl your tongue on caro (expensive), it sounds sluggish; if you use the breathy throat "rh", caro turns into carro (car). Use one quick, light tap of the tongue tip.
Spelling in Read:: single r (KAH-roo, ah-RAH-ruh, PRAH-too, trah-BAH-lyoo, oh-bree-GAH-doo).

11. Syllable-Final "R" and Verb Infinitives (porta, perto, comer, falar, mar)
How to do it: When "r" sits at the end of a syllable before a consonant (porta, perto) or at the very end of a word (comer, falar, mar), Brazilian Portuguese naturally uses two widespread regional pronunciations—both 100% authentic:
- Soft throat breath (coastal/standard): Keep your tongue tip resting flat behind your bottom teeth and release a light, airy puff of air (like a gentle "h").
- Curled-back R (interior/central-south): Curl your tongue tip slightly upward toward the roof of your mouth, just like the American English "r"—the everyday standard across nearly half of Brazil.
In addition, in casual spoken Portuguese, Brazilians very often drop the final "r" completely at the end of verb infinitives (falar -> fah-LAH, comer -> koh-MÊ), keeping the stress firm on that last vowel.
L1 Trap: The real trap for English speakers is letting the "r" swallow and muddy the vowel before it—turning the clear closed "ê" in comer into a muffled English "er" ("koh-MER"), or weakening the open "aw" in porta. Always pronounce the vowel clearly and purely first before adding either the soft breath or the light curled "r".
Spelling in Read:: rh as the default guide (PAWRH-tuh, PEHRH-too, koh-MÊRH, fah-LAHRH, MAHRH), with a light curled "r" or a dropped final "r" on verbs (koh-MÊ, fah-LAH) being equally natural.


12. Clear Light "L" at the Start of a Syllable (lua, lado, falar, bola, beleza)
How to do it: When "l" starts a word or begins a syllable between vowels (lua, lado, falar, bola), press the very tip of your tongue lightly right behind your upper front teeth while keeping the back of your tongue flat and relaxed. The sound is bright, clean, and forward—like the "l" in English "leap" or "light".
L1 Trap: English speakers often pull the back of the tongue up toward the soft palate (creating a heavy, hollow "dark L" like in "pull" or "milk") even between vowels, making falar or bola sound thick and muffled. Keep the tongue forward and light.
Spelling in Read:: l (LOO-uh, LAH-doo, fah-LAHRH, BAW-luh, beh-LÊ-zuh).

13. Syllable-Final "L" Pronounced as "W" (Brasil, sol, papel, alto, mal)
How to do it: Whenever the letter "l" sits at the end of a syllable or at the end of a word (Brasil, sol, papel, alto, mal), it completely loses its tongue-tip contact and turns into a smooth "w" / "oo" vowel glide. Leave your tongue tip resting down and round your lips gently into "w" (*Brasil* rhymes with "you", and *sol* ends like "saw-oo").
L1 Trap: Native English speakers instinctively lift the tongue tip to the ridge behind the top teeth at the end of words like "Brazil", "hotel", or "animal". Touching the roof of your mouth with the tongue on a final "l" immediately sounds foreign in Portuguese.
Spelling in Read:: w (brah-ZEEW, SAHW, pah-PEHW, AHW-too, MAHW).

14. "T" and "D" Before "I" Sound Becoming "TCH" and "DJ" (tia, noite, dia, pedido, onde)
How to do it: In standard Brazilian Portuguese, whenever "t" or "d" is followed by an "i" sound—whether spelled with "i" (tia, dia, pedido) or unstressed final "-e" pronounced as "ee" (noite, onde, tarde)—slide the blade of your tongue slightly back against the roof of your mouth, turning "t" into a crisp "tch" (like "ch" in "cheese") and "d" into a crisp "dj" (like "j" in "jeans").
L1 Trap: English speakers pronounce "ti" and "di" with a hard, dry tongue tap ("TEE-uh", "DEE-uh", "NOY-tee"), or turn a middle "d" into a soft American "r" ("peh-REE-doh"). Always soften "ti" to "tchee" and "di" to "djee".
Spelling in Read:: tchee for ti / final -te (TCHEE-uh, NÔY-tchee) and djee for di / final -de (DJEE-uh, peh-DJEE-doo, Õ-djee).

15. The "NH" Sound vs. Separate "N + Y" (banho, manhã, vinho, dinheiro, tenho)
How to do it: When "n" and "h" appear together (nh), press the flat middle body of your tongue firmly up against the roof of your mouth while letting your voice resonate through your nose, releasing into the next vowel as one single fused sound. It is similar to the "ny" in English "canyon" or "onion", but blended into a single nasal sound rather than two separate parts.
L1 Trap: English speakers break "nh" into a hard "n" followed by a separate "y" sound ("BAHN-yoh", "VEEN-yoh"), adding an extra beat. Keep the middle of your tongue pressed against the roof of your mouth so the nasal flow and glide happen together in one smooth motion.
Spelling in Read:: ny fused inside the syllable (BÃ-nyoo, mã-NYÃ, VEE-nyoo, djee-NYÊ-roo, TẼ-nyoo).

16. The "LH" Sound vs. Separate "L + Y" (filho, trabalho, mulher, olho, velho)
How to do it: When "l" and "h" appear together (lh), press the wide middle part of your tongue flat against the roof of your mouth and let the air and voice flow smoothly around the sides of your tongue into the next vowel as one single fused sound. It is similar to the "lli" in English "million" or "William", blended into one smooth sound rather than two separate parts.
L1 Trap: English speakers either read "lh" as a plain "l" ("FEE-loh") or split it into a hard "l" followed by a separate "y" beat ("FEEL-yoh", "trah-BAHL-yoh"). Keep the middle of your tongue pressed flat against the roof of your mouth so the sound melts directly into the vowel.
Spelling in Read:: ly fused inside the syllable (FEE-lyoo, trah-BAH-lyoo, moo-LYEHRH, Ô-lyoo, VEH-lyoo).

17. Crisp P, T, and K Without a Puff of Air (pato, tempo, casa, tudo, aqui)
How to do it: Pronounce "p", "t", and "k" (or hard "c" before a, o, u) with a clean, dry contact of the lips or tongue and start the vowel immediately, holding back any burst of breath. For "t" and "d" (when not before an "i" sound), touch the very tip of your tongue right against the back of your upper front teeth rather than higher up on the gum ridge.
L1 Trap: At the start of stressed syllables, English speakers shoot out a strong puff of air after "p", "t", and "k" (like in "pie", "tie", "kite"). In Portuguese, that heavy burst of air sounds harsh and explosive; keep the air completely calm and dry.
Spelling in Read:: clean p, t, k with zero extra h (PAH-too, TẼ-poo, KAH-zuh, TOO-doo, ah-KEE).

18. Keeping "D" and "T" Solid Between Vowels (Never Turning Into an "R" Tap) (nada, tudo, pode, cidade, gato)
How to do it: When "d" or "t" (followed by a, o, or u) appears between two vowels (nada, tudo, pode, gato), touch the tip of your tongue firmly behind your upper front teeth to make a clean, solid "d" or "t" sound—never a quick flick.
L1 Trap: American English speakers automatically turn "d" and "t" between vowels into a fast tongue tap that sounds like a soft "r" (turning "ladder" and "latter" into the same sound). In Portuguese, doing this turns nada (nothing) into "nara" and tudo (everything) into "turo". Keep "d" and "t" solid and distinct.
Spelling in Read:: solid d and t (NAH-duh, TOO-doo, PAW-djee, see-DAH-djee, GAH-too).

19. Pure Sissing "S" (S-, SS, Ç, C+E/I) vs. Buzzing "Z" (Single S Between Vowels, Z) (casa vs. caça, mesa, sapo, você, azul)
How to do it: Portuguese strictly separates pure air hissing from throat buzzing:
- Pure "s" (no throat vibration, like a snake hiss): used at the start of a word (sapo), when doubled as "ss" (passo, massa), with "ç" (caça, coração), and with "c" before "e" or "i" (você, cidade).
- Buzzing "z" (vocal cords vibrating like a bee): used whenever a single "s" sits alone between two vowels (casa, mesa, coisa) and for every written "z" (azul, fazer).
L1 Trap: English speakers often confuse single "s" and double "ss" between vowels, or pronounce "ç" with a "k" sound. Changing "s" to "z" changes the word completely: caça (hunt, pure "s") vs. casa (house, buzzing "z").
Spelling in Read:: s for pure hissing (SAH-poo, KAH-suh for caça, voh-SÊ) vs. z for buzzing (KAH-zuh for casa, MÊ-zuh, ah-ZOOW).

20. "S" Before Consonants: Buzzing "Z" vs. Pure "S" (mesmo, desde, os meninos vs. festa, escola)
How to do it: When "s" (or "z") sits at the end of a syllable right before another consonant, its sound automatically matches the consonant that follows it:
- Before a throat-vibrating consonant (b, d, g, v, l, m, n, r), the "s" turns into a buzzing "z" (mesmo = MÊZ-moo, desde = DÊZ-djee, os meninos = ooz meh-NEE-noos).
- Before a breath-only consonant (p, t, k, f, s) or at the end of a pause, it stays a pure hissing "s" (festa = FEHS-tuh, escola = ees-KAW-luh).
L1 Trap: English speakers pronounce "mesmo" with a sharp hissing "s" ("MEHS-moh"), which sounds stiff and choppy. Let your vocal cords start vibrating early so the "s" blends smoothly as a "z" into "m", "d", "l", or "n".
Spelling in Read:: z before vibrating consonants (MÊZ-moo, DÊZ-djee, ooz meh-NEE-noos) vs. s before breath consonants (FEHS-tuh, ees-KAW-luh).

21. The Sounds of "X" and "CH" (SH, KS, Z, S) (chuva, xícara, táxi, exame, próximo)
How to do it: The letters "ch" always sound like a smooth, continuous "sh" (as in English "shoe", never "ch" in "cheese"). The letter "x" changes sound depending on the word:
- Smooth "sh": at the start of a word (xícara) or after "en-" and "me-" (mexer, enxugar).
- Sharp "ks": in words of foreign or Greek origin (táxi, fixo, complexo).
- Buzzing "z": when "ex-" comes at the start of a word followed by a vowel (exame, exemplo, existir).
- Pure hissing "s": in specific common words like próximo, máximo, and texto.
L1 Trap: English speakers instinctively read "ch" with a hard "t" burst at the start ("TCHOO-vuh" instead of "SHOO-vuh") and pronounce "x" as "ks" everywhere ("ek-ZAH-mee" instead of "eh-ZAH-mee"). Never put a "t" sound before Portuguese "ch".
Spelling in Read:: sh (SHOO-vuh, SHEE-kah-ruh), ks (TAHK-see), z (eh-ZÃ-mee), and s (PRAW-see-moo).

22. Smooth Continuous "J" and Soft "G" (Before E/I) vs. Hard English "DJ" (janela, jogo, gente, girar)
How to do it: Pronounce "j" (janela, jogo, hoje) and "g" before "e" or "i" (gente, girar, gelo) as a smooth, continuous buzzing sound with your vocal cords vibrating—identical to the "s" in English "measure", "vision", or "beige". Let the air flow smoothly without blocking your tongue against the roof of your mouth first.
L1 Trap: Native English speakers start "j" and soft "g" by pressing the tongue tip against the roof of the mouth for a hard "d" burst (like in "jam" or "giant"), saying "djah-NEH-luh" or "DJẼ-tchee". In Portuguese, starting with that "d" burst sounds heavy and foreign; keep the tongue tip down so the sound flows smoothly from the very first millisecond.
Spelling in Read:: zh (zhah-NEH-luh, ZHÔ-goo, ZHẼ-tchee, zhee-RAHRH).

23. Silent vs. Pronounced "U" in "QU" and "GU" (queijo, aqui, guerra vs. água, cinquenta, tranquilo)
How to do it: When "q" or "g" is followed by "ue" or "ui", the "u" is normally completely silent—it is just a spelling signal keeping the "k" or hard "g" sound before "e" and "i" (queijo = KÊ-zhoo, aqui = ah-KEE, guerra = GEH-rhuh, guitarra = gee-TAH-rhuh). However, in certain common words (and always before "a" or "o", as in água or quatro), the "u" is pronounced as a quick "w" glide (cinquenta = sĩ-KWẼ-tuh, tranquilo = trã-KWEE-loo, linguiça = lĩ-GWEE-suh).
L1 Trap: English speakers pronounce "qu" as "kw" in every word, saying "KWAY-zhoh" for queijo or "ah-KWEE" for aqui. Unless the word is one of the specific "kw/gw" exceptions, drop the "u" completely and jump straight from "k" or hard "g" into the "e" or "i".
Spelling in Read:: k and g for silent U (KÊ-zhoo, ah-KEE, GEH-rhuh) vs. kw and gw where pronounced (AH-gwuh, sĩ-KWẼ-tuh, trã-KWEE-loo).

24. Always-Silent Initial "H" (hora, hoje, homem, hotel, hospital)
How to do it: At the beginning of a Portuguese word, the letter "h" is 100% silent—it never produces any breath, whisper, or throat sound. Start your voice directly on the vowel that follows it (hora = AW-ruh, hoje = Ô-zhee, homem = Ô-mẽy, hotel = oh-TEHW).
L1 Trap: Because Portuguese shares many cognates with English (hora/hour, hotel/hotel, hospital/hospital, humano/human), native English speakers instinctively breathe out a strong English "h" at the start ("hoh-TEHW", "hohs-pee-TAHW"). Silence the "h" completely and launch straight into the vowel.
Spelling in Read:: Omit the initial h completely (AW-ruh, Ô-zhee, Ô-mẽy, oh-TEHW, ohs-pee-TAHW).

25. Pronounced Initial "PS-"/"PN-" and Light "-EE-" Transition in Medial Consonant Clusters (psicologia, pneu, ritmo, advogado)
How to do it: Unlike English (where the "p" in "psychology" or "pneumonia" is silent), Brazilian Portuguese pronounces every consonant in initial clusters like "ps-" and "pn-" by inserting a quick, light "ee" vowel bridge between them (psicologia = pee-see-koh-loh-ZHEE-uh, pneu = pee-NÊW). The exact same light "ee" cushion appears naturally inside words whenever two consonants meet without a vowel between them, such as "tm", "dv", "bt", or "gn" (ritmo = RHEE-tchee-moo, advogado = ah-djee-voh-GAH-doo, digno = DJEE-gee-noo).
L1 Trap: English speakers either drop the first consonant completely ("sy-koh-loh-ZHEE-uh", "nêw") or slam the consonants together dryly ("REET-moh", "ahd-voh-GAH-doh"). Adding that tiny, unstressed "ee" bridge—which also triggers the "tchee" and "djee" softening on "t" and "d"—is what makes your rhythm sound genuinely Brazilian.
Spelling in Read:: Light unstressed ee bridging the consonants (pee-see-koh-loh-ZHEE-uh, pee-NÊW, RHEE-tchee-moo, ah-djee-voh-GAH-doo).

26. Natural "-EE" Ending on Loanwords Ending in Stop Consonants (internet, rock, hot dog, club, site)
How to do it: Native Portuguese words almost never end in hard stop consonants like "t", "k", "g", "b", or "p". When Brazilian Portuguese uses everyday words borrowed from English (internet, rock, hot dog, club, site), speakers naturally add a quick, unstressed "ee" sound at the very end—which also triggers the "tchee" and "djee" softening when the word ends in "t" or "d" (internet = ĩ-tehrh-NEH-tchee, hot dog = haw-tchee DAW-gee, rock = RHAW-kee).
L1 Trap: Pronouncing these words with your crisp, dry English ending ("in-ter-NET", "hot DOG") sounds abrupt in the middle of a Portuguese sentence. Adding that light final "ee" keeps your speech flowing in natural Brazilian rhythm.
Spelling in Read:: Unstressed ee added to final stop consonants (ĩ-tehrh-NEH-tchee, RHAW-kee, haw-tchee DAW-gee, KLOO-bee, SÃY-tchee).

27. Natural Word Stress Rules vs. Written Accent Marks (falar, Brasil, aqui vs. casa, amigo, café, você)
How to do it: Portuguese has a strict, predictable stress system so you never have to guess which syllable gets the punch:
- Words with a written accent mark (´ or ^) ALWAYS put the stress on that marked syllable—and the mark also tells you whether E/O is open (´ = eh/aw: café, avó) or closed (^ = ê/ô: você, avô).
- Words WITHOUT an accent mark naturally stress the second-to-last syllable when ending in "a", "e", "o", "am", "em", or their plurals (KAH-zuh, ah-MEE-goo, FAH-lãw).
- Words WITHOUT an accent mark ending in "r", "l", "z", "i", "u", "im", "um", or "om" naturally stress the VERY LAST syllable (fah-LAHRH, brah-ZEEW, ah-KEE, ah-RHÔS).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers habitually pull the stress back to the first syllable ("FAH-lar", "BRAH-zil", "AH-kee"), completely distorting the melody of Portuguese. Always push the punch to the final syllable on words ending in -r, -l, -z, -i, or -u unless a written accent tells you otherwise.
Spelling in Read:: UPPERCASE letters marking the exact stressed syllable (fah-LAHRH, brah-ZEEW, ah-KEE vs. KAH-zuh, ah-MEE-goo, kah-FEH, voh-SÊ).

28. Continuous Word Linking Without Throat Stops (um amigo, com ela, os amigos, de água)
How to do it: In Brazilian Portuguese, words link together into one smooth, uninterrupted musical stream. When a word ends with a consonant or nasal sound and the next word starts with a vowel, bridge them directly without pausing or catching your throat:
- Final "-s" or "-z" before a vowel turns into a buzzing "z" linking directly into the next word (os amigos = ooz-ah-MEE-goos, mais ou menos = mãyz-ô-MẼ-noos).
- Final nasal "-m" or "-n" links softly into the following vowel with a gentle nasal bridge (um amigo = ũ-mã-MEE-goo, com ela = kõ-MEH-luh).
- Unstressed "de" before a vowel often blends directly into the next word (de água = DJAH-gwuh).
L1 Trap: English speakers instinctively stop the airflow with a tiny catch in the throat before every word starting with a vowel ("oom... ah-MEE-goh", "koh-m... EH-luh"), breaking the sentence into choppy pieces. Keep your vocal cords humming continuously from one word into the next.
Spelling in Read:: Hyphen linking across word boundaries (ooz-ah-MEE-goos, mãyz-ô-MẼ-noos, ũ-mã-MEE-goo, kõ-MEH-luh, DJAH-gwuh).

es_en
1. The Five Pure Vowels and Even Syllable Rhythm (No Lazy "UH" or Swallowed Syllables) (banana, chocolate, diferente, teléfono)
How to do it: Spanish has only five crisp, pure vowel sounds—A ("ah" as in "father"), E ("eh" as in "bed"), I ("ee" as in "see"), O ("oh" as in "more"), and U ("oo" as in "food")—and every single syllable gets equal, clear time. Keep the same bright vowel quality in unstressed syllables as in stressed ones, giving each written vowel its own clean beat (banana = bah-NAH-nah, chocolate = choh-koh-LAH-teh, diferente = dee-feh-REHN-teh).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers automatically weaken unstressed vowels into a lazy, neutral "uh" sound ("buh-NAH-nuh", "tuh-LEH-fuh-noh") and crush middle syllables in familiar cognates ("CHOC-late", "DIF-rent"). Never swallow a syllable or relax a vowel into "uh"; pronounce every written vowel distinctly.
Spelling in Read:: ah, eh, ee, oh, oo in every syllable with UPPERCASE stress (bah-NAH-nah, choh-koh-LAH-teh, dee-feh-REHN-teh, teh-LEH-foh-noh).

2. Pure Steady "E" and "O" Without English Vowel Slides (café, leche, noche, poco, hola)
How to do it: Keep your jaw, lips, and tongue completely frozen from the beginning of "e" ("eh") and "o" ("oh") until the sound ends. Round your lips firmly for "o" and hold a medium, steady mouth opening for "e", cutting the sound cleanly on a single, unchanging note.
L1 Trap: English speakers naturally slide their jaw and lips upward at the end of long vowels, turning "e" into "ay-ee" (like in "say") and "o" into "oh-oo" (like in "go"). Saying "kah-FAY-ee", "NOH-oo-cheh", or "POH-oo-koh-oo" immediately gives away an English accent. Freeze your mouth on one pure note.
Spelling in Read:: steady eh and oh with zero trailing y or w on pure vowels (kah-FEH, LEH-cheh, NOH-cheh, POH-koh, OH-lah).

3. Pure "U" (Without a Hidden "Y") and Clean Open "A" (universidad, música, útil, gato, mano)
How to do it: For Spanish "u", push your lips forward into a tight, round circle and start the pure "oo" sound directly—just like the "oo" in "food". For Spanish "a", drop your jaw straight down vertically to produce a clean, open "ah" (like the "a" in "father"), keeping the front of your tongue relaxed and flat.
L1 Trap: When English speakers see "u" at the start of words or after consonants (university, music, useful), they instinctively sneak a "y" sound in front ("yoo-nee-ver-see-tee", "MYOO-zik", "YOO-teel"). And for "a", English speakers often pull the corners of the mouth wide into the flat, nasal "a" of "cat" ("GAH-toh" spoken like "gat-oh"). Never add a "y" before "u", and keep "a" open and round.
Spelling in Read:: pure oo and open ah (oo-nee-behr-see-DAHD, MOO-see-kah, OO-teel, GAH-toh, MAH-noh).

4. Smooth Two-Vowel Glides in a Single Beat (AI, EI, OI, AU, EU, IE, UE, UA, IO) (aire, seis, hoy, auto, tiempo, bueno, agua)
How to do it: When a strong vowel (a, e, o) meets a weak vowel (i, u)—or when "i" and "u" meet each other—without a written accent mark, blend both vowels into one single, fast syllable beat. Glide smoothly from the first vowel straight into the second without pausing or adding an extra syllable (aire = AY-reh, tiempo = TYEHM-poh, bueno = BWEH-noh, agua = AH-gwah).
L1 Trap: English speakers tend to split these combinations into two separate, heavy syllables ("ah-EE-reh", "tee-EHM-poh", "boo-EH-noh") or hold the first vowel too long. Keep the weak "i" (sounding like a quick "y") and weak "u" (sounding like a quick "w") tightly fused to the main vowel in one beat.
Spelling in Read:: ay, ey, oy, aw, ehw for falling glides (AY-reh, seys, oy, AW-toh) and y/w fused before the vowel for rising glides (TYEHM-poh, BWEH-noh, AH-gwah).

5. Broken Vowel Pairs With Written Accents (ÍA, ÍO, ÚA, AÍ, AÚ) — Two Separate Beats (día, frío, país, baúl, continúa)
How to do it: Whenever a weak vowel ("i" or "u") next to another vowel carries a written accent mark (í or ú), the accent breaks the blend and forces two completely separate syllable beats. Give the accented "í" or "ú" full stress and voice power, then move cleanly to the neighboring vowel (día = DEE-ah, frío = FREE-oh, país = pah-EES, baúl = bah-OOL).
L1 Trap: English speakers instinctively squash adjacent vowels together into a single syllable, turning país (country) into "pays", día into a single "dyah", or continúa into three beats instead of four. Always respect the written accent mark by splitting the vowels into two distinct beats.
Spelling in Read:: Hyphen separating the two vowel beats with UPPERCASE stress on the accented vowel (DEE-ah, FREE-oh, pah-EES, bah-OOL, kohn-tee-NOO-ah).

6. Identical Double Vowels (EE, OO, AA) — Two Separate Beats (leer, creer, alcohol, zoológico)
How to do it: When two identical vowels sit side by side—such as "ee", "oo", or "aa"—pronounce both vowels as two distinct, separate syllable beats rather than blending them into one long English vowel. Keep your voice steady and lightly re-attack the second vowel (leer = leh-EHR, creer = kreh-EHR, alcohol = ahl-koh-OHL).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers automatically read "ee" as the English "ee" sound in "see" ("leer" as in looking sideways) and "oo" as the English "oo" in "food" ("zoológico" starting like "zoo"). In Spanish, "leer" has two "eh" beats and "zoológico" starts with two "oh" beats (soh-oh-LOH-hee-koh).
Spelling in Read:: Hyphen separating both identical vowels with UPPERCASE stress on the accented beat (leh-EHR, kreh-EHR, ahl-koh-OHL, soh-oh-LOH-hee-koh).

7. Crisp P, T, and K Without a Puff of Air (papá, tiempo, casa, queso)
How to do it: Pronounce "p", "t", and "k" (spelled "c" before a, o, u, or "qu" before e, i) with a clean, dry contact of the lips, tongue, or back of the mouth, releasing directly into the vowel with zero extra breath.
L1 Trap: At the beginning of stressed syllables, English speakers shoot out a strong burst of air after "p", "t", and "k" (like in "pie", "tie", "kite"). In Spanish, that puff of air sounds harsh and explosive; keep the airflow completely calm and dry so "papá" and "tiempo" start cleanly.
Spelling in Read:: Clean p, t, k with no extra h (pah-PAH, TYEHM-poh, KAH-sah, KEH-soh).

8. Tongue-on-Teeth T and D (Never Turning Into an "R" Tap Between Vowels) (todo, nada, cada, patata, bonito)
How to do it: For Spanish "t" and initial or post-consonant "d", touch the very tip of your tongue right against the back of your upper front teeth—slightly further forward than in English—and keep "t" solid and crisp even when it sits between two vowels (patata, bonito).
L1 Trap: American English speakers automatically turn "t" and "d" between vowels into a fast tongue flick that sounds like a soft "r" (making "latter" and "ladder" sound identical). In Spanish, turning "t" into an "r" flick turns bonito into "bo-NEE-roh" and patata into "pah-TAH-rah". Always press the tongue tip firmly behind the top front teeth on "t".
Spelling in Read:: Solid t and d (TOH-doh, NAH-dah, KAH-dah, pah-TAH-tah, boh-NEE-toh).

9. Merged B and V at the Start of Words or After M/N — Both Sound Like Firm B (voy, vaca, bien, vida, un vaso, invierno)
How to do it: In Spanish, the letters "b" and "v" represent the exact same sound. At the very start of a phrase (after a pause) or right after "m" or "n" (which naturally becomes "m" before "b" or "v"), press both lips together firmly and release a clean, solid "b" sound—never touch your top teeth to your bottom lip (voy = boy, vaca = BAH-kah, un vaso = oom BAH-soh, invierno = eem-BYEHR-noh).
L1 Trap: English speakers instinctively bite their lower lip with their upper front teeth whenever they see the letter "v" (saying "voy", "VAH-kah", "een-VYEHR-noh"). In Spanish, biting your lip for "v" is a major giveaway of an English accent; "vaca" and "baca" sound 100% identical.
Spelling in Read:: Always b at the start of a breath group or after m/n (boy, BAH-kah, byehn, BEE-dah, oom BAH-soh, eem-BYEHR-noh).

10. Softened B and V Between Vowels (Lips Almost Touching) (cabeza, libro, saber, vivir, nuevo)
How to do it: Whenever "b" or "v" sits between two vowels—either inside a word (cabeza, libro, vivir, nuevo) or across connected words (la vaca, mi boca)—bring your upper and lower lips close together without pressing them shut or stopping the air. Let the voice and air slip smoothly through the narrow gap between your relaxed lips.
L1 Trap: English speakers either press their lips tightly shut for a heavy, popping "b" ("kah-BEH-sah") or bite their lower lip for an English "v" ("vee-VEER", "NWEH-voh"). Keep both lips relaxed and slightly parted so the sound flows smoothly like a gentle hum.
Spelling in Read:: Softened b (kah-BEH-sah, LEE-broh, sah-BEHR, bee-BEER, NWEH-boh).

11. Softened "D" Between Vowels (Like a Light English "TH") and Soft Final "D" (todo, nada, madre, ciudad, usted, Madrid)
How to do it: Whenever a single "d" sits between two vowels—either inside a word (todo, nada, madre) or across connected words (la duda, mi dinero)—do not press your tongue tip hard against the ridge behind your teeth. Instead, touch the very tip of your tongue lightly against the bottom edge of your upper front teeth, letting the air and voice slip through smoothly—almost identical to a very soft English "th" in "this" or "father". At the very end of a word (ciudad, usted, Madrid), touch the tongue tip lightly behind the top teeth and cut the sound immediately with zero pop.
L1 Trap: American English speakers turn "d" between vowels into a fast tongue flick that sounds like an "r" (saying "TOH-roh" for todo or "NAH-rah" for nada), or hit the "d" too heavily. Softening it toward a gentle "th" keeps your Spanish smooth and natural.
Spelling in Read:: Softened d (TOH-doh, NAH-dah, MAH-dreh, syoo-DAHD, oos-TEHD, mah-DREED).

12. Softened "G" Between Vowels (GA, GO, GU) vs. Hard "G" at the Start or After N (agua, amigo, pagar, luego vs. gato, tengo)
How to do it: When "g" (followed by a, o, or u) sits at the very start of a phrase or right after "n" (gato, tengo, un gato), press the back of your tongue firmly against the soft roof of your mouth to make a crisp, hard English "g" as in "go". However, whenever "g" sits between two vowels (agua, amigo, pagar, luego), relax the back of your tongue so it only comes close to the roof of your mouth without blocking the air, letting a smooth, continuous hum pass through.
L1 Trap: English speakers make a hard, popping "g" in every position ("ah-MEE-goh", "pah-GAHR"). Relaxing the tongue between vowels removes that heavy bump and lets words like agua and amigo flow effortlessly.
Spelling in Read:: g (AH-gwah, ah-MEE-goh, pah-GAHR, LWEH-goh, GAH-toh, TEHN-goh).

13. The Soft Single "R" (One Quick Tongue Tap) (pero, caro, para, tres, grande)
How to do it: When a single "r" appears between two vowels (pero, caro, para) or right after another consonant in the same syllable (tres, grande, libro), flick the very tip of your tongue once against the ridge just behind your upper front teeth. It is the exact same fast tongue tap Americans use for the "tt" or "dd" in "water", "butter", or "ladder".
L1 Trap: Native English speakers instinctively pull and curl the tongue back into the middle of the mouth ("PEH-roh", "TRAYS"), which immediately sounds heavy and foreign. Do not curl your tongue at all—just give one light, fast tap of the tongue tip behind your top front teeth.
Spelling in Read:: single r (PEH-roh, KAH-roh, PAH-rah, trehs, GRAHN-deh).

14. The Rolled "RR" and Word-Initial / Post-Consonant "R" (perro, carro, rosa, rey, alrededor)
How to do it: When "r" is doubled in the middle of a word (perro, carro), sits at the very start of a word (rosa, rey, rápido), or comes right after "n", "l", or "s" (alrededor, Enrique, Israel), rest the relaxed tip of your tongue lightly against the ridge behind your upper front teeth and blow a steady stream of air across it so the tongue tip flutters several times in place—like imitating a purring engine ("brrr").
L1 Trap: English speakers either curl the tongue back into an American "r" ("KAH-roh" instead of "KAH-rroh") or scrape the back of the throat. Never curl your tongue or use your throat—keep the vibration 100% at the loose tip of your tongue behind your upper teeth. Notice that mixing up single "r" and double "rr" changes word meanings completely: pero (but) vs. perro (dog), caro (expensive) vs. carro (car).
Spelling in Read:: rr (PEH-rroh, KAH-rroh, RROH-sah, rrey, ahl-rreh-deh-DOHR).

15. Syllable-Final "R" and Verb Infinitives (-AR, -ER, -IR) Without Curling the Tongue (hablar, comer, salir, puerta, amor)
How to do it: When a single "r" sits at the end of a syllable before a consonant (puerta, tarde) or at the very end of a word (hablar, comer, salir, amor), finish the vowel cleanly and give a crisp, quick tap of your tongue tip against the ridge behind your upper front teeth (or a brief, light flutter when emphasizing the end of a word).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers automatically curl their tongue tip upward and backward at the end of words, letting the "r" swallow and muddy the vowel before it (turning comer into American "koh-MER" or puerta into "PWER-tuh"). Always keep the vowel pure first ("koh-MEHR", "PWEHR-tah") and finish with a forward tongue-tip tap rather than an American curled "r".
Spelling in Read:: r at the end of the syllable or word (ah-BLAHR, koh-MEHR, sah-LEER, PWEHR-tah, ah-MOHR).

16. Always-Clear Light "L" in Every Position (No English "Dark L") (sol, papel, hotel, alto, algo, leche)
How to do it: Keep the tip of your tongue pressed lightly right behind your upper front teeth and keep the back of your tongue flat and relaxed in every position—whether at the start of a word (leche), before a consonant (alto, algo), or at the very end of a word (sol, papel, hotel).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers pull the back of the tongue up toward the soft roof of the mouth at the end of syllables ("soul", "ho-tell", "all-to"), producing a heavy, hollow "dark L" that swallows the vowel before it. Keep the back of your tongue flat so the vowel stays bright and pure right up to the tongue-tip contact.
Spelling in Read:: l (sohl, pah-PEHL, oh-TEHL, AHL-toh, AHL-goh, LEH-cheh).

17. Pure Oral Vowels Before M and N & Natural N Blending (pan, en, cama, un beso, cinco, invierno)
How to do it: Pronounce every vowel before "n" or "m" 100% cleanly through your mouth first, and only move your tongue tip (for "n") or lips (for "m") at the very end of the syllable (pan, en, cama). Also, when "n" comes right before "b", "v", or "p", let your lips close naturally into an "m" (un beso = oom BEH-soh, invierno = eem-BYEHR-noh); before "k" or hard "g" sounds (cinco, tengo), let the back of your tongue touch the roof of your mouth as in English "sing".
L1 Trap: English speakers often let vowels turn nasal early before "n" or "m", or keep a stiff tongue-tip "n" before "v" ("oon VEH-soh"). Keep the vowel completely oral and let "n" naturally adapt to the consonant after it.
Spelling in Read:: Clean vowel + n or m (pahn, ehn, KAH-mah, oom BEH-soh, SEEN-koh, eem-BYEHR-noh).

18. Strong Throat Friction for "J" and Soft "G" (Before E/I) (jamón, gente, hijo, rojo, trabajar)
How to do it: Pronounce "j" in all positions (jamón, hijo, rojo, trabajar) and "g" before "e" or "i" (gente, girar) by raising the back of your tongue slightly toward the soft roof of your mouth and pushing air through the narrow gap to create a crisp, raspy throat friction—similar to gently clearing your throat or the "ch" in Scottish "loch" or German "Bach" (or a firm, airy breathy "h" in softer dialects, never a buzz).
L1 Trap: English speakers instinctively pronounce "j" and soft "g" with a voiced "dj" sound (like in "jam" or "gentle"), or use a weak, barely audible English "h". Never vibrate your vocal cords or block your tongue behind your teeth; keep the sound in the back of your mouth/throat with zero voice buzz.
Spelling in Read:: kh (khah-MOHN, KHEHN-teh, EE-khoh, RROH-khoh, trah-bah-KHAHR).

19. Always-Pure Hissing "S" (Never "Z" Between Vowels) and Pure "S" for "Z" and Soft "C" (casa, mesa, presidente, visitar, zapato, corazón, gracias)
How to do it: In international Spanish, the letter "s", the letter "z", and the letter "c" before "e" or "i" all produce the exact same clean, sharp hissing "s" sound (like the "ss" in "hiss"), with zero vocal-cord buzz—even when a single "s" sits right between two vowels (casa, mesa, presidente, visitar).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers automatically turn a single "s" between vowels into a buzzing "z", especially in familiar cognates ("PREZ-i-dent", "VIZ-it", "MOO-zik", "KAH-zah"). Keep your vocal cords switched off on every "s", "z", and soft "c": casa sounds like "KAH-sah", presidente like "preh-see-DEHN-teh", and corazón like "koh-rah-SOHN".
Spelling in Read:: Always s (KAH-sah, MEH-sah, preh-see-DEHN-teh, bee-see-TAHR, sah-PAH-toh, koh-rah-SOHN, GRAH-syahs).

21. The "Ñ" Sound as One Single Fused Sound (Never Split Into "N + Y") (niño, español, mañana, año)
How to do it: Press the flat middle body of your tongue firmly up against the roof of your mouth while letting your voice resonate through your nose, releasing directly into the next vowel in one single fused motion—similar to the "ny" in English "canyon" or "onion", but blended together rather than split into two beats.
L1 Trap: English speakers break "ñ" into a hard "n" followed by a separate "y" beat ("NEEN-yoh", "mahn-YAH-nah"), adding an extra syllable pulse. Keep the middle of your tongue pressed flat against the roof of your mouth so the nasal sound and glide happen together.
Spelling in Read:: ny fused inside the syllable (NEE-nyoh, ehs-pah-NYOHL, mah-NYAH-nah, AH-nyoh).

22. "LL" and "Y" Pronounced as a Smooth "Y" (Never "L" or Heavy "J") (calle, pollo, yo, playa, ella)
How to do it: In modern international Spanish, double "ll" and the letter "y" (before a vowel) share the exact same sound: bring the middle of your tongue gently close to the roof of your mouth and let the voice flow smoothly like the English "y" in "yes" or "yellow" (calle = KAH-yeh, pollo = POH-yoh, ella = EH-yah).
L1 Trap: English speakers either read "ll" as an "l" sound ("KAH-leh", "POH-loh") or push it too hard into an English "j" sound ("joh" for yo, "EH-jah" for ella). Keep the sound light and smooth like English "yes".
Spelling in Read:: y (KAH-yeh, POH-yoh, yoh, PLAH-yah, EH-yah).

23. Silent vs. Pronounced "U" in "QU", "GU", and "GÜ" (que, quien, guerra, guitarra vs. agua, pingüino, vergüenza)
How to do it: Whenever "q" is followed by "ue" or "ui" (que, quien, aquí), the "u" is always 100% silent—it just signals a crisp "k" sound (keh, kyehn, ah-KEE). Similarly, in "gue" and "gui" (guerra, guitarra), the "u" is silent and keeps the "g" hard like in "go" (GEH-rrah, gee-TAH-rrah). However, before "a" or "o" (agua, antiguo) or when two dots appear over the "ü" (pingüino, vergüenza), pronounce the "u" as a quick "w" glide (AH-gwah, peen-GWEE-noh, behr-GWEHN-sah).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers instinctively pronounce "qu" as "kw" in every word, saying "kweh" for que or "ah-KWEE" for aquí. Drop the "u" completely in "que", "qui", "gue", and "gui" unless you see the two dots (ü).
Spelling in Read:: k and g for silent U (keh, kyehn, GEH-rrah, gee-TAH-rrah) vs. gw where pronounced (AH-gwah, peen-GWEE-noh, behr-GWEHN-sah).

24. Always-Silent Letter "H" (Including English Cognates) (hola, hacer, hijo, ahora, hotel, hospital)
How to do it: In Spanish, the letter "h" is 100% silent in every single position—at the start of a word or in the middle. Never breathe out or make any throat friction; launch your voice directly on the vowel that follows it (hola = OH-lah, hacer = ah-SEHR, ahora = ah-OH-rah).
L1 Trap: Because Spanish shares many cognates with English (hotel/hotel, hospital/hospital, humano/human, horrible/horrible), English speakers automatically breathe out an English "h" ("hoh-TEHL", "hohs-pee-TAHL", "HOH-lah"). Silence the "h" completely in every word.
Spelling in Read:: Omit the letter h completely (OH-lah, ah-SEHR, EE-khoh, ah-OH-rah, oh-TEHL, ohs-pee-TAHL).

25. Keeping the Initial "E-" in "ES- + Consonant" Words and Silent "P" in "PS-" (español, escuela, estar, estudiar, psicología)
How to do it: Spanish words never start directly with "s + consonant"—they always place a clear, full "e" vowel in front (español, escuela, estar, estudiar). Pronounce that opening "eh" beat cleanly every time. Conversely, when a word starts with "ps-" (psicología, psicólogo), the "p" is silent in everyday speech and the word starts directly with a hissing "s" (see-koh-loh-KHEE-ah).
L1 Trap: Because English has "Spanish", "school", and "study" without an initial "e", native English speakers frequently swallow or clip the opening "e-" in Spanish ("spah-NYOHL", "SKWEH-lah", "stahr"). Give that opening "eh-" its own clear beat.
Spelling in Read:: Full opening eh- syllable (ehs-pah-NYOHL, ehs-KWEH-lah, ehs-TAHR, ehs-too-DYAHR) and direct s for ps- (see-koh-loh-KHEE-ah).

26. The Sounds of the Letter "X" (KS, S, and Throat "KH" in México) (examen, taxi, texto, explicar, México)
How to do it: The letter "x" changes sound depending on its position in the word:
- Between vowels (examen, taxi, éxito): pronounce it as a crisp "ks" (ehk-SAH-mehn, TAHK-see, EHK-see-toh).
- Before a consonant (texto, explicar, extranjero): simplify it to a pure hissing "s" (TEHS-toh, ehs-plee-KAHR).
- In México and mexicano: pronounce it with the raspy throat friction "kh" of Spanish "j" (MEH-khee-koh, meh-khee-KAH-noh).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers voice "x" as "gz" in cognates like exam ("eg-ZAM" -> saying "ehg-ZAH-mehn") or read México with a "ks" sound ("MEK-see-koh"). Keep the vocal cords off in examen ("ehk-SAH-mehn") and use the throat "kh" in México.
Spelling in Read:: k-s between vowels (ehk-SAH-mehn, TAHK-see), s before consonants (TEHS-toh, ehs-plee-KAHR), and kh in México (MEH-khee-koh).

27. Natural Word Stress Rules, Written Accents, and Cognate Traps (hablo vs. habló, esta vs. está, animal, hospital, policía)
How to do it: Spanish stress is 100% predictable by looking at the last letter of the word:
- Words WITHOUT a written accent ending in a vowel (a, e, i, o, u), "n", or "s" ALWAYS stress the second-to-last syllable (KAH-sah, HAH-bloh, ehs-TOO-dyahn).
- Words WITHOUT a written accent ending in any other consonant (such as r, l, d, z) ALWAYS stress the VERY LAST syllable (ah-BLAHR, ah-nee-MAHL, ohs-pee-TAHL, syoo-DAHD, feh-LEES).
- Whenever a word breaks those two rules, Spanish puts a written accent mark (´) on the exact syllable that gets the punch—and moving that stress often changes the entire meaning (HAH-bloh = I speak vs. hah-BLOH = he/she spoke; EHS-tah = this vs. ehs-TAH = is; PAH-pah = potato vs. pah-PAH = dad).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers instinctively pull the stress to the first syllable in familiar cognates ("AN-i-mal", "HOS-pi-tal", "HO-tel") or ignore written accents. Always push the punch to the final syllable on words ending in -l, -r, -d, or -z unless an accent mark tells you otherwise.
Spelling in Read:: UPPERCASE letters marking the exact stressed syllable (ah-nee-MAHL, ohs-pee-TAHL, poh-lee-SEE-ah, HAH-bloh vs. hah-BLOH).

28. Continuous Word Linking Without Throat Catches (la amiga, va a hablar, de él, los amigos)
How to do it: In spoken Spanish, words flow together as one continuous stream with zero pauses between them:
- When a word ends in a consonant and the next word starts with a vowel, link the consonant directly into the vowel as if they were one word (los amigos = loh-sah-MEE-gohs, el hombre = eh-LOHM-breh).
- When two different vowels meet across words, glide them smoothly together in a single beat (de él = dehl, mi amigo = myah-MEE-goh).
- When two identical vowels meet across words, merge them into one single, steady vowel beat (la amiga = lah-MEE-gah, va a hablar = bah-BLAHR).
L1 Trap: English speakers instinctively insert a tiny catch in the throat before every word starting with a vowel ("lah... ah-MEE-gah", "lohs... ah-MEE-gohs"), breaking the sentence into choppy pieces. Keep your airflow and voice humming continuously across word boundaries.
Spelling in Read:: Hyphen linking across word boundaries (loh-sah-MEE-gohs, eh-LOHM-breh, lah-MEE-gah, bah-BLAHR).



fr_en
1. The French "U" ("ü") vs. "OU" ("oo") (tu, rue, une vs. vous, dessous, dessus)
How to do it: To make the French single "u" sound (tu, rue, une, dessus), shape your tongue to say a bright English "ee" (as in "see"), keep your tongue tip resting behind your bottom front teeth, and push your lips forward into a tight, small whistling circle as if saying "oo". In contrast, when spelled "ou" (vous, dessous, tout), pull your tongue back and round your lips for a pure, deep "oo" (as in "food").
L1 Trap: Native English speakers either pronounce French "u" just like English "oo" (confusing tu = you with tout = all, or dessus = above with dessous = below) or sneak a "y" sound in front ("tyoo", "ryoo", "yoon"). Never add a "y" sound, and keep your tongue forward for "u" vs. back for "ou".
Spelling in Read:: ü for single u (tü, rhü, ün, duh-SÜ) vs. oo for ou (voo, duh-SOO, too).

2. Closed "EU" and "ŒU" ("ø") (deux, peu, bleu, vœu, yeux)
How to do it: When "eu" or "œu" sits at the end of a word (peu, bleu, vœu) or before a silent final consonant or buzzing "z" sound (deux, heureuse), position your tongue as if saying a pure, steady "ay" (without sliding upward at the end), and round your lips forward into a tight "oh" circle while holding that exact tongue position.
L1 Trap: English speakers pronounce "eu" as a flat, unrounded "uh" (like "duh" for deux) or as "oo" ("doo", "bloo"). Rounding your lips firmly while keeping the tongue forward is what creates this authentic French vowel.
Spelling in Read:: ø (dø, pø, blø, vø, yø).

3. Open "EU", "Œ", and "ŒU" ("œ") (sœur, cœur, jeune, peur, heure)
How to do it: When "eu", "œ", or "œu" is followed by a pronounced consonant in the same syllable—especially "r", "l", "f", or "n" (sœur, cœur, jeune, peur, heure)—drop your jaw slightly more open than for closed "ø". Position your tongue for a clean "eh" (as in "bed") while rounding your lips gently into an "aw" shape.
L1 Trap: Native English speakers curl their tongue back on words ending in "-eur" (sœur, peur, heure), turning them into an American English "er" sound ("sir", "purr", "her"). Keep your tongue tip down behind your bottom teeth and round your lips instead of curling your tongue.
Spelling in Read:: œ (sœrh, kœrh, zhœn, pœrh, œrh).

4. The Neutral "E" ("uh") — Kept in One-Syllable Words vs. Dropped Inside Words (le, je, de, me, ce vs. petit, samedi, fenêtre)
How to do it: An unaccented "e" at the end of tiny one-syllable words (le, je, de, me, ce, ne, que) has a quick, light, slightly rounded "uh" sound—similar to the "u" in "put" with gently rounded lips. However, when an unaccented "e" sits in the middle of a word between two single consonants (petit, samedi, fenêtre, maintenant), native speakers drop it completely in everyday speech so the surrounding consonants meet cleanly (petit = ptee, samedi = sahm-DEE, fenêtre = fneh-trh).
L1 Trap: English speakers either pronounce every written "e" heavily ("puh-TEET", "sah-muh-DEE", "fuh-NEH-truh"), making their French sound robotic and slow, or flatten the "e" in "le/je/de" into an unrounded "lay/jay". Keep short words lightly rounded and drop the middle "e" when it sits cleanly between consonants.
Spelling in Read:: uh for kept neutral e (luh, zhuh, duh, muh, suh) and omitted where naturally dropped (ptee, sahm-DEE, fneh-trh).

5. Pure Closed "É / -ER / -EZ" ("ay") vs. Open "È / Ê / AI / EI" ("eh") (parlé, café, chez vs. parlais, lait, fête, merci)
How to do it: French sharply separates two "e" sounds:
- Closed "ay" (spelled é, final -er in verbs, final -ez, or es in short words like les/des): hold your tongue high and tense for a pure, steady "ay" sound without sliding upward at the end (parlé = pahr-LAY, café = kah-FAY, chez = shay).
- Open "eh" (spelled è, ê, ai, ei, or e before two consonants): drop your jaw cleanly to make an open "eh" sound as in "bed" (parlais = pahr-LEH, lait = leh, fête = feht, merci = mehr-SEE).
L1 Trap: English speakers automatically slide closed "ay" into "ay-ee" ("kah-FAY-ee") and blur the difference between closed and open "e"—which changes verb tenses completely: parlé (spoken, closed "ay") vs. parlais (was speaking, open "eh"). Freeze your mouth on closed "ay" and drop your jaw for open "eh".
Spelling in Read:: ay for steady closed E (pahr-LAY, kah-FAY, shay) vs. eh for open E (pahr-LEH, leh, feht, mehr-SEE).

6. Pure Steady "AU" and "EAU" ("oh") Without Sliding Into "OO" (beau, eau, auto, chaud, bateau, restaurant)
How to do it: Whenever you see the letter combinations "au" or "eau" (as well as "ô" or final "-ot"), round your lips firmly into a tight circle and hold a single, pure, steady "oh" sound from start to finish—without moving your lips or jaw while the sound plays (beau = boh, eau = oh, auto = oh-TOH, chaud = shoh, bateau = bah-TOH).
L1 Trap: Native English speakers naturally slide their lips tighter at the end of "oh" (like in "go" or "boat"), turning it into a two-part "oh-oo" sound ("boh-oo", "bah-TOH-oo"), or read "au" as "aw" ("AW-toh"). Freeze your lips in one fixed round shape for a clean, single-note "oh".
Spelling in Read:: oh (boh, oh, oh-TOH, shoh, bah-TOH, rehs-toh-RAHN).
